    • PrahladAnS SB 10-05-11 - Exercise Of Hearing - 1997-04-19 Radhadesh
      His Holiness Prahladananada Swami· 1997-04-19· English
    Personal Guidance
    The gopīs' radiant rush to greet Kṛṣṇa shows that true bhakti is the whole self — body, ornaments, and heart — moving toward Him in eager love.
    Practical Applications
    • Before any seva or darśana today, prepare yourself with the same care the gopīs took — clean clothes, clean body, clean intention — refusing to approach Kṛṣṇa carelessly.
    • Notice when 'going to greet Kṛṣṇa' (temple, japa, study) feels like an obligation, and deliberately walk into it with the gopīs' eagerness instead.
    • Offer one tangible thing today — a flower, a meal, a small gift — at Kṛṣṇa's altar, imitating the gopīs who never came empty-handed.
    Reflection Questions
    • The gopīs adorned themselves not for display but to welcome Kṛṣṇa — what care do I take in my own preparation (body, dress, mood) before approaching Him in sādhana, and where have I let that preparation become careless or self-centered?
    • These eternal associates moved together toward Nanda's house with shared jubilation — where in my life am I trying to walk toward Kṛṣṇa in solitary self-importance instead of with the saṅga of devotees, and what ahaṅkāra is that protecting?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Before posting a selfie or curating an outfit, pause and ask honestly: am I dressing for Kṛṣṇa, for my friends' approval, or for the algorithm? The gopīs dressed elaborately — but every bangle was for Him. Try one day this week where you get ready as if Kṛṣṇa is the only audience.
    • Notice how your friend group reacts to news. When something exciting drops in the group chat, you all run to it together. The gopīs ran together to Nanda's house at the news of Kṛṣṇa. Find one friend who will run with you toward kīrtana, a class, or a temple visit the way you currently run toward concerts and parties.
    • Your phone, your room, your backpack are all 'adornments' you curate. Today, place one tulasī leaf, one image of Kṛṣṇa, or one small offering somewhere visible — not as decoration, but as a daily reminder that the externals can be tuned toward Him without becoming fake or performative.
    householder
    • When you dress for work, a wedding, or a family gathering, reframe one element of your preparation as offered to Kṛṣṇa — the tilaka before the meeting, the tulasī mālā under the shirt, the prayer before stepping out. The gopīs' ornaments were elaborate but never ego-centered; the same is possible in your professional life.
    • Decisions about your children's clothes, school events, and birthday parties are constant. Ask: am I performing for other parents, or am I raising children who will run toward Kṛṣṇa with the gopīs' jubilation? Choose one upcoming family decision where you consciously prioritize the second over the first.
    • When good news comes — a promotion, a baby, a house — your first instinct is to call family or post it. Before any of that, offer the news at the altar, the way the gopīs went straight to Nanda's house. Make Kṛṣṇa the first recipient of every important update for one full month and notice what it does to the household.
    senior
    • Loosen attachment to your own jewelry, saris, watches, and keepsakes by giving one item this month to a younger devotee — not as inheritance, but as a living offering to their service of Kṛṣṇa. The gopīs' ornaments were never theirs; they were always His.
    • At this stage the body's adornments fade — hair thins, joints stiffen, mirrors are less kind. Use this as an invitation, not a loss. The gopīs' beauty was never the body's; it was the radiance of one running toward Kṛṣṇa. Each morning, do one small act of preparation (bathing, tilaka, fresh cloth) explicitly as your offering.
    • Mentor a younger devotee or grandchild this week by telling them, in your own voice, the news of Kṛṣṇa's appearance — a pastime from the Bhāgavatam, a memory of your guru, a moment from your own sādhana. Be the one who carries the news to Nanda's house for the next generation.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Reading the gopīs' ornaments as mere material vanity and dismissing the verse — missing that their decoration is an offering, an expression of prema, not a display of viṣaya.
    • Treating the gopīs as ordinary village women and so flattening the verse into folklore, instead of recognizing them as expansions of Kṛṣṇa's pleasure potency whose every gesture is non-different from devotional service.
    • Imitating the gopīs' externals — their bhāva, their rasa, their intimacy — as a sādhana shortcut, when our actual stage calls for vinaya, vaidhī-bhakti, and the humility of one still cleaning the mirror of the heart.
    Discussion Prompts
    • The gopīs adorned themselves elaborately before going to see Kṛṣṇa — earrings, bangles, garlands, colored cloth. Is bodily adornment for Kṛṣṇa's pleasure compatible with vairāgya, or does it contradict the teaching that the body is not the self? Where does your group actually disagree?
    • Prabhupāda insists the gopīs are not ordinary women but expansions of Kṛṣṇa's pleasure potency (hlādinī-śakti). In pairs, work out the practical difference between hearing this verse as 'a charming village scene' versus hearing it as 'eternal associates of the Lord in Goloka.' Does the difference change anything you would do tomorrow?
    • The verse shows the gopīs running together to Nanda's house at the news of Kṛṣṇa's appearance. Discuss: when news of Kṛṣṇa reaches you — a class, a kīrtana, a festival — do you respond with the gopīs' urgency, or with householder calculation? Push each other on the honest answer, not the pious one.
    Role Play

    A new student arrives at your nāma-hatta program dressed in plain street clothes. She sees several devotees in fresh dhotīs and saris, tilaka, neck-beads, flowers in their hair, and quietly asks you afterwards: 'I thought bhakti was internal. Why all the dressing up? Isn't this just another form of vanity, only with a Kṛṣṇa label on it?' One student plays the new seeker; another plays the senior devotee responding. A third observes and calls out any answer that drifts toward either (a) impersonalism ('it's all just symbolic, the externals don't matter') or (b) mere ritualism ('we do it because we're supposed to'). The teacher then opens the floor: how do we, like the gopīs, dress the body for Kṛṣṇa's pleasure rather than our own — and how can the listener tell the difference?

    Objections & Responses
    • This verse is just a lavish description of women's jewelry and bodies — earrings, breasts, garlands, hair. It reads like a male poet's fantasy, not spiritual teaching. Why should serious students dwell on it?
      Read it as you would read the description of a Deity in the temple. Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam describes the gopīs' ornaments because the gopīs themselves are Kṛṣṇa's eternal associates — ānanda-cinmaya-rasa-pratibhāvitābhiḥ, expansions of His pleasure potency, as Brahma-saṁhitā says. Their bodies are not material in the ordinary sense; their adornments are not for self-display but for Kṛṣṇa's pleasure. Treating the verse as erotic spectacle is precisely the misreading Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu warned against — ramyā kācid upāsanā vrajavadhū-vargeṇa yā kalpitā: there is no higher worship than that conceived by the gopīs. The verse trains the heart to see ornament-as-offering rather than ornament-as-vanity, which is a real distinction we have lost in modern life.
    • Devotion should be inward. The gopīs' jubilant rush, the showers of flowers from their hair, the bangles and lockets — this all sounds like religious enthusiasm that crosses into emotionalism. Isn't a steady, sober sādhana more mature?
      Steadiness and jubilation are not opposites in the Gauḍīya line; they are stages. The dhīra-bhāva of BG 2.13 keeps you anchored when the body changes. The gopīs' jubilation here is not sentimentality — it is the natural response of eternal associates at the news of Kṛṣṇa's appearance. Cold sobriety in the face of Kṛṣṇa's pastimes is not maturity; it is anartha, a covering over the soul's natural rasa. Prabhupāda did not teach a dry, stoic bhakti. He taught chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa with feeling, dancing in kīrtana, weeping for separation. The corrective to emotionalism is not impersonal flatness; it is sva-rūpa-siddhi — emotion rightly directed to Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, never to an impersonal Absolute or to one's own ego.
    Analogy

    Look at a procession of devotees moving toward the temple at festival hour — every step shaking loose a small rain of garlands, every bangle ringing the bell that arrival has not yet rung. The gopīs going to Nanda's house are this procession in its eternal form: motion itself becoming worship.

    Stories
    Caitanya's saṅkīrtana through Navadvīpa

    Caitanya-caritāmṛta describes how Mahāprabhu's saṅkīrtana parties moved through Navadvīpa with such intensity that flowers, garlands, and ornaments scattered from the dancing devotees' bodies onto the streets.

    Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī, glossing such passages, points to verses like SB 10.5.11 as their archetype: in Vraja, the very act of moving toward Kṛṣṇa is so saturated with adornment and ecstasy that the path itself is decorated by the movers. Caitanya recovered this mood for Kali-yuga.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.5.12

    ↗
    ता आशिष: प्रयुञ्जानाश्चिरं पाहीति बालके । हरिद्राचूर्णतैलाद्भ‍ि: सिञ्चन्त्योऽजनमुज्जगु: ॥ १२ ॥
    tā āśiṣaḥ prayuñjānāś
    [taa aashishah prayunjaanaash]
    ciraṁ pāhīti bālake
    [chiram paaheeti baalake]
    haridrā-cūrṇa-tailādbhiḥ
    [haridraa-choorna-tailaadbhih]
    siñcantyo ’janam ujjaguḥ
    [sinchantyo ’janam ujjaguh]
    Word-for-word
    tāḥ — all the women, the wives and daughters of the cowherd men āśiṣaḥ — blessings prayuñjānāḥ — offering ciram — for a long time pāhi — may You become the King of Vraja and maintain all its inhabitants iti — thus bālake — unto the newborn child haridrā - cūrṇa — powder of turmeric taila - adbhiḥ — mixed with oil siñcantyaḥ — sprinkling ajanam — the Supreme Personality of Godhead, who is unborn ujjaguḥ — offered prayers .
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)

    offered prayers .
    Important Words
    • āśiṣaḥ — blessings
      From ā + √śās ('to instruct, to bless'). The same root as śāstra and śāsana. Elder women's āśiṣas in the Vedic world carry real weight — they are not pleasantries but invocations. The gopīs' blessing is a true coronation.
    • ciram-pāhi — may you long protect (us)
      Imperative of √pā ('to protect') with ciram ('long'). The cowherd women are not asking that Kṛṣṇa be protected — they are asking Him to protect Vraja, even as a newborn. The grammar already reveals His identity as the protector.
    • haridrā-cūrṇa-taila-adbhiḥ — with turmeric powder, oil, and water
      Three auspicious substances in one compound. This combination is the traditional abhiṣeka mixture still used in Indian weddings, births, and royal consecrations. The līlā is rooted in living vidhi.
    • ajanam — the unborn Supreme Personality of Godhead
      A + jana ('not born'). The verse names Him 'unborn' precisely while celebrating His birth — Gauḍīya doctrine: Kṛṣṇa's appearance (āvirbhāva) is not ordinary janma. He is ajaḥ — without material birth — even as He becomes Yaśodā's son. Acintya.
    • ujjaguḥ — they sang aloud (offered prayers)
      Ud + √gai. The prefix ud ('up, out') intensifies — the gopīs' prayers are not murmured but lifted up. Vraja's voice rises to its newborn king.
    Offering blessings to the newborn child, Kṛṣṇa, the wives and daughters of the cowherd men said, “May You become the King of Vraja and long maintain all its inhabitants.” They sprinkled a mixture of turmeric powder, oil and water upon the birthless Supreme Lord and offered their prayers.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Offering blessings to the newborn child, Kṛṣṇa, the wives and daughters of the cowherd men said, "May You become the King of Vraja and long maintain all its inhabitants." They sprinkled a mixture of turmeric powder, oil and water upon the birthless Supreme Lord and offered their prayers.
    Commentary

    Entering the maternity ward they said "May you protect us." This means that, being the king’s son, when he becomes king, he should protect them. Another reading is "Ciran jiva, live long life," said out of parental affection as a blessing. Then going out side, they sprinkled each other with turmeric and oil mixed with water, and began singing in loud voices.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    In joy they said “Please protect us for a long time.” By that they prayed that he remain wealthy for all time, and prayed that he associate with them and be pleased with them. Bālake means “unto a very small child.” They prayed in this way because he was a helpless child, or they prayed out of great prema, even though he was a small baby. They loudly (ut) sang auspicious songs or they praised him so that he would be safe. Instead of pāhi sometimes jīva (live) is seen, uttered out of compassion. Not only did the women give blessings. Everyone did so.

      His Holiness Prahladananada Swami· 1997-04-21· English
    • ISKCON Kolhapur SB 10-05-12 Hindi - Nandotsava Katha - 2008-08-30 HG Radha Gopinath Prabhu
      ISKCON Kolhapur· 2008-08-30· Hindi
    Personal Guidance
    The gopīs bless the aja (birthless) Kṛṣṇa as their future king — true blessing flows when we serve Him as a Person, not an abstraction.
    Practical Applications
    • Offer a small, concrete blessing to someone in your household today, asking Kṛṣṇa — by name — to protect and maintain them.
    • Refuse to reduce Kṛṣṇa to 'the Self' or 'the divine' in your speech or thought; address Him personally, as the gopīs did.
    • Notice one moment today when you receive care from elders or community, and consciously trace it back to Kṛṣṇa as its source.
    Reflection Questions
    • When I bless those younger than me — children, juniors, students — am I praying for their bodily comfort and worldly success, or for their connection to Kṛṣṇa as the gopīs prayed 'ciraṁ pāhi' (long maintain)?
    • The verse calls Kṛṣṇa 'ajanam' — the birthless — even as the women treat Him as a newborn to be protected. Where in my own bhakti am I willing to hold both: tender, hands-on service to Him, and full awareness that He needs nothing from me?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • When a friend posts a milestone — a new job, an admit, a relationship — actually show up with a genuine blessing (a call, a handwritten note, a small gift), not a one-tap emoji. Practice dāna the way the gopīs did: presence, not performance.
    • Before your next exam or interview, ask an elder in your family — grandparent, aunt, parent — for an old-fashioned blessing. Receive it without irony. Notice how your nervous system settles; that is śānti the tradition has been transmitting for centuries.
    • When you feel pressure to flex achievements on social media, recall that the gopīs poured turmeric on the Lord and asked nothing for themselves. Post one thing this week that blesses someone else's win instead of staging your own.
    householder
    • At your next family milestone — a child's birthday, a housewarming, a new job — resist the rajas-pull toward expensive spectacle. Plan one element that mirrors the gopīs: simple ingredients, sincere prayers, and gifts to those who serve (a teacher, a cook, an elder), turning celebration into dāna.
    • Begin or revive a small daily blessing of your children — a hand on the head before school, a prayer over them as they sleep. You are not making them safe; Kṛṣṇa does that. You are practicing vātsalya in the mood of Vraja and giving them a memory that will outlive you.
    • When making a big family decision (school, move, money), invite a senior or a Vaiṣṇava well-wisher to formally bless the choice before you act. Treat their words as the gopīs' āśiṣaḥ — not magic, but tyāga of the illusion that you alone are the doer.
    senior
    • Make a deliberate practice of blessing the young — grandchildren, students, neighbors' children — out loud, with their name and a prayer for Kṛṣṇa's shelter over them. This is the elder's dāna, and it is what the gopīs modeled for every generation since.
    • Loosen one attachment this month by giving away something you have been holding 'for later' — a piece of jewelry, a book collection, savings — to someone who will use it in Kṛṣṇa's service. Practice tyāga while you can still feel the pinch; that is the seniors' satya-test.
    • Sit with the word ajanam — the birthless one. The body that was once an infant blessed by elders is now the elder doing the blessing, and soon it will be neither. Each evening, rehearse leaving it; each morning, accept it again as a tool for service. Equanimity (samatā) is the fruit.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Reading the gopīs' affectionate care for the 'newborn' Kṛṣṇa as sentimentality, and missing that the verse explicitly names Him ajanam — the birthless Supreme Lord — so their mood is reverence-in-intimacy, not mere maternal feeling.
    • Imitating the vātsalya-bhāva of Vraja by treating Kṛṣṇa as a baby in one's own practice, when one has not earned that adhikāra — instead of beginning where the śāstra places us, in dāsya and reverence.
    • Reducing 'blessings and prayers' to a generic well-wishing exercise, forgetting that the gopīs' prayer was specific: that He become King of Vraja and protect its inhabitants — a prayer rooted in His personal identity and relationships, not an impersonal 'send good vibes.'
    Discussion Prompts
    • The gopīs blessed Kṛṣṇa with 'May You long maintain Vraja' — yet He is the birthless Lord who needs no protection. Share with your partner: when have you 'blessed' or worried over someone whose welfare was, in truth, far beyond your reach? Was the gesture foolish, or was it bhakti? Where do you actually disagree on this in the group?
    • Nanda Mahārāja gives lavishly to brāhmaṇas and the cowherd women pour turmeric, oil, and water on the newborn — pure dāna in a festival of joy. In small groups, argue both sides honestly: is generous celebration of a child's birth (or any milestone) today an act of dāna and śānti, or has it slid into ego-display and rajas? Where is the line for your own family?
    • The verse calls Kṛṣṇa ajanam — the birthless one — even while the gopīs treat Him as a fragile newborn. Discuss: does this 'as if' relationship soften your bhakti into sentimentality, or sharpen it into discriminative wisdom? Pair up with someone who leans the opposite way from you and don't settle for a polite middle.
    Role Play

    Set the scene: a young couple in your saṅga has just had a baby. The grandmother insists on a traditional blessing ceremony — turmeric, oil, sprinkling of water, gifts to elders, prayers for long life — modeled on what the gopīs did for Kṛṣṇa. The husband, a recent reader of the Gītā, objects: 'The soul is eternal and birthless. This is superstition. Let's just chant and skip the rituals.' The wife is torn. Assign three students: grandmother, husband, wife. A fourth student plays a visiting Vaiṣṇava elder who must, in five minutes, help the family see how the gopīs themselves modeled the very ceremony in question — and why dismissing it as 'superstition' confuses impersonalism with bhakti. Debrief: where did the 'elder' lean too hard on tradition versus too hard on philosophy?

    Objections & Responses
    • This is just folk superstition dressed up as scripture — sprinkling turmeric water on a baby and calling him a king. How is this any different from any village blessing ritual anywhere in the world?
      A Gauḍīya teacher would say: notice who is doing the sprinkling and on whom. These are not anxious villagers performing magic on an unknown infant — these are the gopīs of Vraja, the most exalted devotees, performing acts of love on the Supreme Personality of Godhead, who they know in their hearts as their own. The outward form resembles a folk rite; the inner substance is pure rāgānugā affection. Bhakti does not abolish loving custom — it fills it with Kṛṣṇa. Strip the form away in the name of philosophy and you are left not with higher truth but with a colder heart. The same hands that sprinkle turmeric also offer the soul.
    • If Kṛṣṇa is ajanam — birthless — and the gopīs know He is the Supreme Lord, why pray for His long life and protection? It's incoherent. Either He is God and needs nothing, or He is a baby who does. Pick one.
      A Gauḍīya teacher would say: this is precisely the sweetness (mādhurya) the verse points to. Kṛṣṇa's greatness is that He accepts the role of a dependent child so His devotees can serve Him in vātsalya-rasa — parental love. The gopīs' 'incoherence' is not a logical failure; it is the supreme triumph of bhakti over abstract theology. To love Kṛṣṇa as Lord-and-only-Lord is real but limited; to love Him as your own little boy whom you must protect — that is what He came to Vṛndāvana to enjoy. The personalist line honors both: He is the Absolute, and He is the child being bathed in turmeric. Refuse the second and you refuse half of who He is.
    Analogy

    When a king is crowned, the elder women of the court sprinkle him with the auspicious mixture of turmeric and oil — not because the king needs cleansing but because the act seals him into their care. The gopīs sprinkling the unborn Lord with haridrā-taila are crowning Kṛṣṇa not as monarch of an empire but as cira-pāhi — the eternal King of Vraja.

    Stories
    Rāma's coronation interrupted

    In the Rāmāyaṇa, the women of Ayodhyā prepared the abhiṣeka substances for Rāma's coronation — turmeric, oil, sacred waters — only for the ceremony to be cancelled by Kaikeyī's intervention. The crowning had to wait fourteen years.

    The contrast is instructive: Rāma's outer coronation was delayed; Kṛṣṇa's coronation as Vrajeśvara begins at birth, in the gopīs' hands, with the same haridrā and taila — and this coronation is never cancelled. Vraja is the land where the Lord is crowned by love at His very arrival.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.5.13

    ↗
    अवाद्यन्त विचित्राणि वादित्राणि महोत्सवे । कृष्णे विश्वेश्वरेऽनन्ते नन्दस्य व्रजमागते ॥ १३ ॥
    avādyanta vicitrāṇi
    [avaadyanta vichitraani]
    vāditrāṇi mahotsave
    [vaaditraani mahotsave]
    kṛṣṇe viśveśvare ’nante
    [krishne vishveshvare ’nante]
    nandasya vrajam āgate
    [nandasya vrajam aagate]
    Word-for-word
    avādyanta — vibrated in celebration of Vasudeva’s son vicitrāṇi — various vāditrāṇi — musical instruments mahā - utsave — in the great festival kṛṣṇe — when Lord Kṛṣṇa viśva - īśvare — the master of the entire cosmic manifestation anante — unlimitedly nandasya — of Mahārāja Nanda vrajam — at the pasturing place āgate — had so arrived .
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)
    avādyanta vicitrāṇi
    avādyanta
    vibrated in celebration of Vasudeva’s son
    vicitrāṇi
    various
    vāditrāṇi mahotsave
    vāditrāṇi
    musical instruments
    mahā - utsave
    in the great festival
    kṛṣṇe viśveśvare ’nante
    kṛṣṇe
    when Lord Kṛṣṇa
    viśva - īśvare
    the master of the entire cosmic manifestation
    anante
    unlimitedly
    nandasya vrajam āgate
    nandasya
    of Mahārāja Nanda
    vrajam
    at the pasturing place
    āgate
    had so arrived .
    Important Words
    • avādyanta — were vibrated in celebration
      Imperfect passive of ā + √vad ('to sound'). The instruments are the grammatical subjects — they 'were sounded' as if Vraja itself were playing them. The līlā is participatory; even the inanimate joins.
    • kṛṣṇe — in Lord Kṛṣṇa
      Locative singular. The Sanskrit construction (locative absolute) reads 'Kṛṣṇa having arrived...' — the entire celebration is grammatically anchored in His arrival. Without Him, no festival; with Him, everything sounds.
    • viśva-īśvare — the master of the entire cosmic manifestation
      Viśva + īśvara. Even in this most intimate Vraja moment, Śukadeva will not let us forget who Kṛṣṇa is. Gauḍīya doctrine: svayaṁ-bhagavān is simultaneously the cosmic Lord and the village child — the acintya tension is the rasa.
    • anante — the unlimited one
      An + anta ('without end'). The infinite has just become finite enough to be cradled in Vraja. The juxtaposition with the small wooden cot is exactly the wonder the verse means us to feel.
    • vrajam-āgate — having arrived at Vraja
      Locative absolute with āgata ('come'). Vraja is here the location of the Lord's arrival, not merely a place He visits — He has come home to where He eternally is. The Gosvāmīs' theology of nitya-Vraja rests on such grammatical anchors.
    Now that the all-pervading, unlimited Lord Kṛṣṇa, the master of the cosmic manifestation, had arrived within the estate of Mahārāja Nanda, various types of musical instruments resounded to celebrate the great festival.
    Purport

    The Lord says in Bhagavad-gītā (4.7) : yadā yadā hi dharmasya glānir bhavati bhārata abhyutthānam adharmasya tadātmānaṁ sṛjāmy aham “Whenever and wherever there is a decline in religious practice, O descendant of Bharata, and a predominant rise of irreligion — at that time I descend Myself.” Whenever Kṛṣṇa comes, once in a day of Brahmā, He comes to the house of Nanda Mahārāja in Vṛndāvana. Kṛṣṇa is the master of all creation ( sarva-loka-maheśvaram ). Therefore, not only in the neighborhood of Nanda Mahārāja’s estate, but all over the universe — and in all the other universes — musical sounds celebrated the auspicious arrival of the Lord.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Now that the all-pervading, unlimited Lord Kṛṣṇa, the master of the cosmic manifestation, had arrived within the estate of Mahārāja Nanda, various types of musical instruments resounded to celebrate the great festival.
    Commentary

    Instruments began playing in the three worlds—svarga, earth and hell, because Krsna, the Lord of the universe (visvesvara), had appeared. Because Krsna is unlimited, unlimited instruments began to sound.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Instruments were played by musicians. There were various instruments such as vīṇas, drums, flutes, karatālas and gongs. Besides the variety of instruments, there was variety in how the musicians played. When the musicians of Vraja played, all others in the universe also played spontaneously. The reason is given. This was at a festival greater than any other (mahotsave). The reasons for the festival are given. Kṛṣṇa, Bhagavān, had personally appeared with the glory of his attractive qualities. He had all influence (viśveśvare) and had unlimited power and sweetness (anante). He had appeared in his own place, the supreme place endowed with an ocean of nectar of the highest prema (nandasya vrajam). Instead of īyūṣi (appeared) sometimes āgate (came) is seen.

    Related values & verses
    Related values
    Vairagya (Detachment / Dispassion)Discriminative WisdomSeva (Service / Selfless Action)Kshama (Forgiveness / Patience)Atma Nigraha (Self-control)

    Concepts
    Chit
    Related lectures
    • Padaseva Pr SB 10-05-13 Hindi
      His Grace Padaseva Prabhu· Hindi
    Personal Guidance
    When Kṛṣṇa enters the home, the whole cosmos resounds — invite Him into your day and let even ordinary moments become utsava.
    Practical Applications
    • Before beginning your work today, formally welcome Kṛṣṇa into your space with a short prayer or offering, treating His arrival as a real event.
    • Notice one ordinary moment — a meal, a doorway, a greeting — and turn it into a small mahotsava by remembering Kṛṣṇa is the unseen guest.
    • Refuse to treat Kṛṣṇa as a distant Absolute today; speak to Him personally as Nanda's son, not as 'the universe' or 'consciousness.'
    Reflection Questions
    • When Kṛṣṇa 'arrives' in some corner of my life — a moment of clarity, a chance to serve, a turn toward bhakti — what kind of 'musical instruments' do I sound, or do I let the moment pass in silence?
    • Where am I treating Kṛṣṇa as a local, private comfort rather than the viśveśvara — the master of all worlds whose arrival is meant to reorder my entire universe, not just decorate it?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Treat Janmāṣṭamī (or the next Ekādaśī, or your morning japa) the way Vraja treated Kṛṣṇa's arrival — actually mark it. Skip a class party that conflicts, cook something, invite one friend; don't let it pass as just another date on your phone calendar.
    • Notice what you currently 'sound instruments' for — exam results, a crush's reply, a scholarship, an Instagram post going well. List three. Ask honestly: would I bring half that excitement to a kīrtana? Begin closing that gap this week.
    • When peers mock devotional enthusiasm as 'cringe,' remember the vrajavāsīs were not embarrassed. You don't have to evangelize, but don't hide your sādhana to seem cool — that hiding is itself a vote for whose advent matters.
    householder
    • Make Kṛṣṇa's 'arrival' visible in the home — a deity room, a daily ārati your children attend even if briefly, festival days that interrupt the work-school rhythm. The home should know when the Lord has come; otherwise the calendar belongs entirely to office and school.
    • When career milestones, promotions, or a child's achievement occur, hold a small offering before the celebration — let the household learn that even worldly mahotsavas are framed by His presence, not the other way around.
    • Audit the household's sound-track: which 'vāditrāṇi' actually play here — news, streaming, market updates, or kīrtana, śloka recitation, Bhāgavatam? You don't have to silence the first, but ensure the second has real airtime, not just leftover minutes.
    senior
    • At this stage, every arrival of the holy name in your day is rarer and more precious than ever — guard the morning sādhana fiercely; it is your festival, and the body's complaints are not grounds to cancel it.
    • Become a vrajavāsī for the next generation: when grandchildren or younger devotees visit, let them encounter someone whose face lights up at Kṛṣṇa's name. That visible delight is the 'vāditrāṇi' you can still play, and it teaches more than any lecture.
    • Reframe the loosening of attachments — retirement, reduced mobility, lost friends — as the universe quieting down so you can hear the instruments that announce His advent at the moment of departure. Practice now what you will need then: chanting clearly, taking shelter, fearing nothing.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Reducing the festival at Nanda's vraja to sentimental nostalgia — enjoying the sweetness of the pastime while ignoring that the same all-pervading Lord is asking for that celebration in your daily sādhana now.
    • Confusing outer festivity (kīrtana volume, decorations, social gathering) with the inner welcome of Kṛṣṇa — making noise without actually receiving Him into the 'estate' of one's heart and household.
    • Privatizing Kṛṣṇa's advent — thinking 'He came for the devotees in Vṛndāvana' as a closed historical event, and missing that the same Lord, anante, descends into every sincere life and deserves a festival there too.
    Discussion Prompts
    • The verse describes the entire universe celebrating Kṛṣṇa's arrival at Nanda's estate. Share with your partner: when Kṛṣṇa 'arrives' in your sādhana — through japa, kīrtana, prasāda — does anything in your life actually 'sound musical instruments,' or does the rest of your day proceed unchanged? Where is the disconnect, and is it a problem?
    • Prabhupāda says Kṛṣṇa descends 'once in a day of Brahmā' — an unimaginably rare event — yet Nanda's neighbors knew it was a festival. In our age, how do we recognize the Lord's presence without the universal soundtrack? Disagree honestly: is the holy name sufficient, or do we secretly want the cosmic spectacle?
    • Vraja celebrated openly with vicitrāṇi vāditrāṇi — varied, public, exuberant music. Many of us practice bhakti privately, almost apologetically, in modern secular spaces. Where is the line between mādhurya-bhāva intimacy and a quiet impersonalist drift that hides Kṛṣṇa from public life? Push back on each other.
    Role Play

    Two students are roommates. Student A has just returned from a Janmāṣṭamī festival at the temple — flushed, joyful, wanting to play kīrtana recordings, cook prasāda, and tell the story of Kṛṣṇa's appearance. Student B is an agnostic classmate cramming for a midterm, who finds the noise distracting and the enthusiasm slightly embarrassing in their shared apartment. A wants the room to be like Vraja when Kṛṣṇa arrived; B wants quiet study. Role-play the conversation. The teacher pauses after five minutes and asks: how does A honor Kṛṣṇa's arrival without imposing? How does B respond to genuine festivity versus mere noise? What would a Gauḍīya practitioner-householder model here?

    Objections & Responses
    • This sounds like mythological exaggeration — the whole universe resounding with music because one baby is born in one village? It's poetic license, not anything I should take literally or apply to my life.
      The verse is precise, not exaggerated. Prabhupāda points out Kṛṣṇa is sarva-loka-maheśvara — the master of all realms — so His advent is a cosmic event, not a local one. The 'exaggeration' reading assumes Kṛṣṇa is one finite character among many; the verse assumes He is the source. The application is not to verify the cosmology by measurement but to ask: do I treat His advent (in the holy name, in the deity, in the ācārya's instruction) as a cosmic event or as background ambience? The verse trains the heart to sense the magnitude that the gross senses cannot.
    • Why does Krishna need a festival, instruments, and celebration? Isn't true devotion silent and inward? All this pageantry feels like it's about the devotees performing their feelings, not about God.
      Gauḍīya bhakti is not impersonalist quietism — it is rasa, relationship. Kṛṣṇa is a person, and persons are honored with gifts, music, food, and embrace, not with abstraction. The vrajavāsīs played instruments because that is how love behaves when its beloved arrives; suppressing it in the name of 'inwardness' is closer to dry jñāna than to bhakti. Inward stillness has its place in śānta-rasa, but the higher rasas — dāsya, sakhya, vātsalya, mādhurya — overflow outward. The festival is not performance for an audience; it is the natural overflow of prema meeting its object.
    Analogy

    When the master of the house comes home after a long journey, every instrument that has been silent for the season comes out of its case — the conch, the flute, the drum. Kṛṣṇa's arrival in Vraja is such a homecoming, and the bherīs and dundubhis are simply the instruments of the dhāma waking up to play their proper Lord home.

    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.5.14

    ↗
    गोपा: परस्परं हृष्टा दधिक्षीरघृताम्बुभि: । आसिञ्चन्तो विलिम्पन्तो नवनीतैश्च चिक्षिपु: ॥ १४ ॥

    Important Words
    • parasparam — on one another
      Para ('other') + spara (reciprocal). The joy is constitutively mutual — no one in Vraja celebrates Kṛṣṇa alone. Bhakti's natural form is saṅga, throwing yogurt at one another in shared abandon.
    • hṛṣṭāḥ — being pleased
      Past participle of √hṛṣ ('to thrill, to bristle with joy'). Gauḍīya literature uses hṛṣṭa for the physical thrill of horripilation that accompanies high bhāva — the gopas are not merely happy; they are bristling with the body's signs of prema.
    • dadhi — curd
      The signature substance of Vraja's dairy economy. The same dadhi Kṛṣṇa will steal as a child is here being thrown joyfully at His birth — the līlā is announcing its own future in the substances of the celebration.
    • ghṛta-ambubhiḥ — with water mixed with ghee
      Ghṛta + ambu. Even the water is enriched with ghee — Vraja's economy of abundance permits the cowherds to throw what elsewhere would be precious. The waste is theological: love does not count.
    • navanītaiḥ ca — and with butter
      Navanīta: 'newly churned.' The verse adds the qualifier that the butter is fresh — same butter that Yaśodā will one day churn, same butter Kṛṣṇa will famously steal. Every detail in 10.5 quietly seeds what is coming in 10.8 onward.
    In gladness, the cowherd men enjoyed the great festival by splashing one another’s bodies with a mixture of curd, condensed milk, butter and water. They threw butter on one another and smeared it on one another’s bodies.
    Purport

    From this statement we can understand that five thousand years ago not only was there enough milk, butter and curd to eat, drink and cook with, but when there was a festival it would be thrown about without restriction. There was no limit to how extensively milk, butter, curd and other such products were used in human society. Everyone had an ample stock of milk, and by using it in many varied milk preparations, people would keep good health in natural ways and thus enjoy life in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    In gladness, the cowherd men enjoyed the great festival by splashing one another's bodies with a mixture of curd, condensed milk, butter and water. They threw butter on one another and smeared it on one another's bodies.
    Commentary

    In a joking mood they began energetically throwing (ciksipatuh) the milk yoghurt, ghee and water on each other . Cikispuh can mean they began to fall on the slippery earth, since ksip means moving the feet as well as the hands.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    In joy they completely (ā) sprinkled each other with yogurt. Anointing each other completely (vi) with butter, they also threw butter, or forcibly covered each other with it. They slipped around because the ground became moist.

    Personal Guidance
    Kṛṣṇa's birth turns ordinary abundance — milk, curd, butter — into joyful offering; real prosperity is measured by what we can give away in His remembrance.
    Practical Applications
    • Take one ordinary abundance in your life today (food, time, skill) and use it lavishly in Kṛṣṇa's service rather than hoarding it.
    • Cook or share a milk-based preparation with family or devotees today, offering it to Kṛṣṇa first and remembering Nanda Mahārāja's festival.
    • Refuse the modern instinct to measure wealth by what you accumulate; ask instead what you have given away in bhakti this week.
    Analogy

    When the river overflows its banks at the height of the monsoon, the villagers do not weep at the loss of order — they laugh and splash and let the abundance soak them. The cowherds throwing yogurt and butter on each other are such villagers, drenched by the flood of vātsalya-rasa that Kṛṣṇa's birth has set loose in Vraja.

    Stories
    Caitanya's Herā-pañcamī and dadhi-cidā

    Caitanya-caritāmṛta (Antya 10) describes Mahāprabhu and His devotees engaging in the dadhi-cidā festival at the Pāṇihāṭi feast hosted by Raghava Paṇḍita and Raghunātha dāsa — devotees splashing each other with curd, beating chipped rice, lost in ecstatic abandon.

    Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja explicitly identifies such Caitanya-līlās as the recovery of Vraja-style utsava in Kali-yuga. The cowherds' butter-splashing in SB 10.5.14 is the archetype; Caitanya's saṅkīrtana festivals are its return. The Gauḍīya cycle of utsavas through the year keeps this thread alive.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.5.15-16

    नन्दो महामनास्तेभ्यो वासोऽलङ्कारगोधनम् । सूतमागधवन्दिभ्यो येऽन्ये विद्योपजीविन: ॥ १५ ॥ तैस्तै: कामैरदीनात्मा यथोचितमपूजयत् । विष्णोराराधनार्थाय स्वपुत्रस्योदयाय च ॥ १६ ॥

    Important Words
    • mahā-manāḥ — great-minded
      Recurs from verse 1 — the chapter brackets Nanda's charity with this epithet. Mahā-manas is a settled disposition, not a momentary mood. The grammar reminds us: Nanda's generosity in verse 16 is the same heart that performed jāta-karma in verse 2.
    • go-dhanam — wealth of cows
      Go + dhana. The compound itself reveals Vraja's economic theology: wealth is not gold; wealth is cows. The Gauḍīya emphasis on go-rakṣaṇa (cow protection) draws from such verses for its scriptural ground.
    • adīna-ātmā — whose self is undepressed, magnanimous
      A + dīna + ātmā: 'not poor in spirit.' Even as Nanda gives lavishly, his ātmā does not contract — the verse insists that real generosity is impossible from a poor-spirited heart. Aparigraha and adīnatā are paired virtues.
    • viṣṇoḥ ārādhana-arthāya — for the purpose of worshiping Lord Viṣṇu
      The clinching phrase. All of Nanda's charity is named here as Viṣṇu-ārādhana — Gauḍīya doctrine: every act in a devotee's life is finally directed to Bhagavān's pleasure. Nanda does not give to relieve poverty alone; he gives to please Viṣṇu through serving His creatures.
    • sva-putrasya-udayāya — for the improvement of his own son
      Udaya: 'rising, flourishing.' The dāna is also for Kṛṣṇa's wellbeing. The double motive (Viṣṇu's worship + son's flourishing) reveals the Gauḍīya truth that Bhagavān and the devotee's beloved are not different — serving Kṛṣṇa as Viṣṇu and as Yaśodā's son is one act.
    The great-minded Mahārāja Nanda gave clothing, ornaments and cows in charity to the cowherd men in order to please Lord Viṣṇu, and thus he improved the condition of his own son in all respects. He distributed charity to the sūtas, the māgadhas, the vandīs, and men of all other professions, according to their educational qualifications, and satisfied everyone’s desires.
    Purport

    Although it has become fashionable to speak of daridra-nārāyaṇa, the words viṣṇor ārādhanārthāya do not mean that all the people satisfied by Nanda Mahārāja in this great ceremony were Viṣṇus. They were not daridra, nor were they Nārāyaṇa. Rather, they were devotees of Nārāyaṇa, and by their educational qualifications they would satisfy Nārāyaṇa. Therefore, satisfying them was an indirect way of satisfying Lord Viṣṇu. Mad-bhakta-pūjābhyadhikā ( Bhāg. 11.19.21). The Lord says, “Worshiping My devotees is better than worshiping Me directly.” The varṇāśrama system is entirely meant for viṣṇu-ārādhana, worship of Lord Viṣṇu. Varṇāśramācāravatā puruṣeṇa paraḥ pumān/ viṣṇur ārādhyate ( Viṣṇu Purāṇa 3.8.9). The ultimate goal of life is to please Lord Viṣṇu, the Supreme Lord. The uncivilized man or materialistic person, however, does not know this aim of life. Na te viduḥ svārtha-gatiṁ hi viṣṇum ( Bhāg. 7.5.31 ). One’s real self-interest lies in satisfying Lord Viṣṇu. Not satisfying Lord Viṣṇu but instead attempting to become happy through material adjustments ( bahir-artha-māninaḥ ) is the wrong way for happiness. Because Viṣṇu is the root of everything, if Viṣṇu is pleased, everyone is pleased; in particular, one’s children and family members become happy in all respects. Nanda Mahārāja wanted to see his newborn child happy. That was his purpose. Therefore he wanted to satisfy Lord Viṣṇu, and to satisfy Lord Viṣṇu it was necessary to satisfy His devotees, such as the learned brāhmaṇas, māgadhas and sūtas. Thus, in a roundabout way, ultimately it was Lord Viṣṇu who was to be satisfied.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The great-minded Mahārāja Nanda gave clothing, ornaments and cows in charity to the cowherd men in order to please Lord Viṣṇu, and thus he improved the condition of his own son in all respects. He distributed charity to the sūtas, the māgadhas, the vandīs, and men of all other professions, according to their educational qualifications, and satisfied everyone's desires.
    Commentary

    Mahamanah means very generous. Nanda Maharaja, who was very generous gave to all who came. Vidyopajivah refers to those who made a living by dancing, singing and play instruments, weapons and scripture etc. Tais tais kamaih means whatever was desired or asked. He gave suitably according to the level of their reputation and knowledge. The purpose of the charity was to worship Visnu in order to please Visnu, and for the auspiciousness of his son. "May Visnu be pleased by this charity, and by his pleasure, my son will be blessed."

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    He gave (verb understood) cloth and other items such as gold to the cowherd men and women, to the bards, and to singers and musicians as appropriate. He worshipped them appropriately. He was naturally generous but now became most devoted (mahāmanāḥ). As previously mentioned, because of his devotion to Kṛṣṇa, his qualities of generosity and wisdom increased immeasurably. He was not satisfied with giving, but whatever they desired he gave. He worshipped them according to caste, age and knowledge. He respected them with garlands, sandalwood, betel nut, and invitations. The purpose of giving is mentioned. It was for satisfying Viṣṇu not for going to Svarga, since he was the highest Vaiṣṇava and had reached the end of desiring any object. He gave for the welfare of his son. May Viṣṇu be pleased with this charity! May my son be prosperous by his mercy! By worshipping Viṣṇu, by that prosperity, there would be manifestation of Kṛṣṇa’s form, qualities and pastimes in his house. Mahāmanāḥ can also mean that his meditation became great because of developing attraction to Kṛṣṇa. Śukadeva has established the conclusion by the meaning of this verse, for the word ca means api to indicate Nanda’s rare position of getting Kṛṣṇa as his son. This established in later verses: nandaḥ kim akarod brahman śreya evaṁ mahodayam yaśodā ca mahā-bhāgā papau yasyāḥ stanaṁ hariḥ O learned brāhmaṇa, mother Yaśodā’s breast milk was sucked by the Supreme Lord. What past auspicious activities did she and Nanda Mahārāja perform to achieve such perfection in ecstatic love? SB 10.8.46 nemaṁ viriṣco na bhavo na śrīr apy aṅga-saṁśrayā prasādaṁ lebhire gopī yat tat prāpa vimuktidāt Neither Lord Brahmā, nor Lord Śiva, nor even the goddess of fortune, who is always the better half of the Supreme Lord, can obtain from the Supreme Lord, the deliverer from this material world, such mercy as received by mother Yaśodā. SB 10.9.20 yuvāṁ ślāghyatamau nūnaṁ dehinām iha māna-da nārāyaṇe ’khila-gurau yat kṛtā matir īdṛśī O respectful Nanda, certainly you and mother Yaśodā are the most praiseworthy persons in the entire world, since you have developed such a loving attitude toward Nārāyaṇa, the spiritual master of all living beings. SB 10.46.30

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    He gave (verb understood) cloth and other items such as gold to the cowherd men and women, to the bards, and to singers and musicians who made their living by their skills, as appropriate. He worshipped them appropriately. He was naturally generous but now became most devoted (mahāmanāḥ), knowing what they desired most or what was known as most excellent (tais taiḥ kāmaiḥ). Or he gave not only to those who made a living by their skills but also to the most unfortunate (anye). He gave not only gifts but respected everyone (ca). He worshipped all the brāhmaṇas and others. According to their birth and knowledge he satisfied their desires because he was generous. Or he worshipped them with desires which bring the Lord under control. That cannot be understood even by ātmārāmas, in order to satisfy the Lord. The plural “desires” is used to indicate respect or many desires. He worshipped for auspiciousness in this life and the next at the birth of his son, out of great affection, in order to satisfy Viṣṇu, since he was the best of devotees. Or he worshipped at the birth of his son for the highest goal (artha)--worshipping Viṣṇu. Actually the result of worshipping Viṣṇu was the birth of his son.

    Personal Guidance
    Pleasing Kṛṣṇa's devotees according to their qualification is the direct way to please Him — and in pleasing Him, your family prospers.
    Practical Applications
    • Identify one devotee or learned servant of Kṛṣṇa in your life and honor them today with a concrete gift, service, or word of appreciation — mad-bhakta-pūjā is higher than direct worship.
    • Refuse the daridra-nārāyaṇa confusion: when you give in charity, give to those whose qualification actually serves Viṣṇu, not merely to soothe your own sentiment about poverty.
    • Before acting for your child's, spouse's, or family's welfare today, first do one act explicitly aimed at satisfying Kṛṣṇa — and trust that their udaya follows from His ārādhana.
    Analogy

    A king's victory feast is incomplete until every guest has received something. Nanda Mahārāja does not simply celebrate Kṛṣṇa's birth privately — he turns the celebration outward, becoming the channel through which Bhagavān's pleasure (viṣṇoḥ ārādhana) reaches every layer of society at his door.

    Stories
    Yudhiṣṭhira's Rājasūya distribution

    After the Rājasūya yajña (Bhāgavatam 10.74–75; Mahābhārata Sabhā Parva), King Yudhiṣṭhira distributed wealth, cloth, ornaments, and food to brāhmaṇas, sūtas, māgadhas, vandīs, and 'all the rest' — the verse-language is almost identical to SB 10.5.15–16.

    The Gauḍīya reading: the model Vaiṣṇava king performs charity not in a hierarchy that excludes anyone, but across the whole society, viṣṇoḥ ārādhana-arthāya — for the pleasure of Viṣṇu. Yudhiṣṭhira and Nanda both show that personal celebration finds completion only in universal sharing.

    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Important Words
    • rohiṇī — Rohiṇī, mother of Baladeva
      From √ruh ('to rise, to grow'). Her name signals upward growth — she is the mother of the brother whose growth Kṛṣṇa Himself protects. The Gauḍīya tradition reveres her as the human form of the eternal Vraja-mother in Balarāma's vātsalya-rasa.
    • mahā-bhāgā — most fortunate
      Mahā + bhāga ('share, portion, fortune'). Rohiṇī's fortune is specifically named — she gets to raise Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma together. The Gauḍīya tradition treats this as one of the highest fortunes available in any creation.
    • nanda-gopā-abhinanditā — honored by Nanda and Yaśodā
      Abhi + √nand: 'rejoiced over.' The same root as Nanda's name. Rohiṇī is welcomed by the Nanda household with the same rasa that gives the household its name — vātsalya extends to the co-mother.
    • vyacarat — was wandering here and there
      Imperfect of vi + √car. The verb suggests active, busy circulation — Rohiṇī is not a guest; she is co-hosting. Her motion through the house establishes her as full kin, not honored visitor.
    • divya — beautiful, divine
      Divya: belonging to the heavenly realm. Even in her domestic role at the festival, Rohiṇī's dress is divya — a quiet reminder that the women of Vraja are not ordinary villagers but eternal pārṣadas.
    The most fortunate Rohiṇī, the mother of Baladeva, was honored by Nanda Mahārāja and Yaśodā, and thus she also dressed gorgeously and decorated herself with a necklace, a garland and other ornaments. She was busy wandering here and there to receive the women who were guests at the festival.
    Purport

    Rohiṇī, another wife of Vasudeva’s, was also kept under the care of Nanda Mahārāja with her son Baladeva. Because her husband was imprisoned by Kaṁsa, she was not very happy, but on the occasion of Kṛṣṇa-janmāṣṭamī, Nandotsava, when Nanda Mahārāja gave dresses and ornaments to others, he also gave gorgeous garments and ornaments to Rohiṇī so that she could take part in the festival. Thus she also was busy receiving the women who were guests. Because of her good fortune in being able to raise Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma together, she is described as mahā-bhāgā, greatly fortunate.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The most fortunate Rohiṇī, the mother of Baladeva, was honored by Nanda Mahārāja and Yaśodā, and thus she also dressed gorgeously and decorated herself with a necklace, a garland and other ornaments. She was busy wandering here and there to receive the women who were guests at the festival.
    Commentary

    Rohini is addressed as most fortunate, maha bhago, because among all of Vasudeva’s wives, she alone attained the opportunity to see Krsna’s childhood pastimes. Nanda gopa means Nanda raja, for gopa also means king, according to the Amara Kosa dictionary. (go means earth as well as cow. Gopa means protector of the earth.) Abhinandita (greeted) means that Nanda praised her saying, "because of your coming here, my child was born." Vyacarat indicates that she went around, giving proper respect to the gathered women. She was decorated with beautiful clothing and ornaments given by Nanda and Yasoda. Because of the bliss of celebrating Krsna’s birth, she forgot her sorrow of her husband’s condition in prison and separation from him.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Rohiṇī means “she who produces (rohayati) great bliss.” Now the meaning of her name had reached perfection. She was had greater fortunate than Vasudeva’s other wives and even Devakī, because she attained the sweet pastimes of Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma by the birth of Balarāma to her and the birth Kṛṣṇa to her dear friend Yaśodā. Nanda, king of Gokula welcomed her by saying “Only by your coming to Vraja have I been able to have a son.” The word gopa can mean king. Or she was welcomed not only by Nanda but by the other cowherds (gopā). She was decorated with cloth rare to this earth and ornaments given by Nanda out of joy, and she forgot all sorrows by his joy. She moved about (vyacarat) here and there with affection doing various tasks at this great festival. Because of her joy at Kṛṣṇa’s birth, there no mention of Rohiṇī’s new born child sleeping in her house.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Rohiṇī was especially honored since she suffered separation from Vasudeva. She was most fortunate because she experienced the appearance of the Lord, and was never separated from Kṛṣṇa, and because she lived in Vraja. She was honored by Nanda, the protector of cows and earth (gopa), a great king. Or she was honored by Nanda and the cowherds. Or mahābhāgāḥ can refer to Yāśodā or all the cowherd women. Or Rohiṇī was honored or made completely happy by Yāśodā and the other women, the cowherds and Nanda. Though she did not want it, because of separation, she was ornamented with good cloth and decorations. Other decorations are also indicated. Yāśodā and others gave her cloth superior to what she was wearing (divya). She moved about (vyacarat) here and there with affection, doing various tasks at this great festival. Because of her joy at Kṛṣṇa’s birth, there is no mention of Rohiṇī’s newborn child sleeping in her house.

      His Holiness Bir Krishna Goswami· 2019-03-15· English
    Personal Guidance
    Even in separation from her husband, mahā-bhāgā Rohiṇī served Kṛṣṇa's festival with full heart — true fortune is participation in His līlā, not personal circumstance.
    Practical Applications
    • Today, dress and show up fully for a service to Kṛṣṇa even while carrying private grief — refuse to let personal sorrow withdraw you from devotional duty.
    • Notice one guest, neighbor, or family member you can honor today as Rohiṇī honored Nanda's visitors — receive them as part of Kṛṣṇa's festival, not as an interruption.
    • Refuse to measure your fortune by external circumstance; instead, name aloud one way Kṛṣṇa has placed you near Him today and call that your mahā-bhāgya.
    Analogy

    When two flames are kept in one lamp, neither casts a separate shadow — they brighten one room together. Rohiṇī and Yaśodā in Nanda's house are such a single lamp: Balarāma and Kṛṣṇa side by side, two mothers serving one household, the elder co-wife not a rival but a kin-flame.

    Stories
    Rohiṇī's quiet motherhood

    Rohiṇī's situation is rare in the śāstra. Her son Balarāma was transferred from Devakī's womb into hers (SB 10.2) and raised in Vraja alongside Kṛṣṇa. She is therefore biologically Baladeva's mother but had to mother him in another woman's house, in another man's household.

    The Bhāgavatam (and Caitanya-caritāmṛta's reverence for Baladeva) honors her without dramatizing her hardship — she is mahā-bhāgā precisely because she carries this complex motherhood with grace, wandering through Nanda's festival 'busy here and there,' indistinguishable from Yaśodā as host. Vraja-vātsalya makes co-mothers, not co-wives.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.5.18

    ↗
    तत आरभ्य नन्दस्य व्रज: सर्वसमृद्धिमान् । हरेर्निवासात्मगुणै रमाक्रीडमभून्नृप ॥ १८ ॥
    tata ārabhya nandasya
    [tata aarabhya nandasya]
    vrajaḥ sarva-samṛddhimān
    [vrajah sarva-samriddhimaan]
    harer nivāsātma-guṇai
    [harer nivaasaatma-gunai]
    ramākrīḍam abhūn nṛpa
    [ramaakreedam abhoon nripa]
    Word-for-word
    tataḥ ārabhya — beginning from that time nandasya — of Mahārāja Nanda vrajaḥ — Vrajabhūmi, the land for protecting and breeding cows sarva - samṛddhimān — became opulent with all kinds of riches hareḥ nivāsa — of the residence of the Supreme Personality of Godhead ātma - guṇaiḥ — by the transcendental qualities ramā - ākrīḍam — the place of pastimes for the goddess of fortune abhūt — became nṛpa — O King (Mahārāja Parīkṣit) .
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)
    tata ārabhya nandasya
    tataḥ ārabhya
    beginning from that time
    nandasya
    of Mahārāja Nanda
    vrajaḥ sarva-samṛddhimān
    vrajaḥ
    Vrajabhūmi, the land for protecting and breeding cows
    sarva - samṛddhimān
    became opulent with all kinds of riches

    hareḥ nivāsa
    of the residence of the Supreme Personality of Godhead

    ramākrīḍam abhūn nṛpa
    ramā - ākrīḍam
    the place of pastimes for the goddess of fortune
    abhūt
    became
    nṛpa
    O King (Mahārāja Parīkṣit) .
    Important Words
    • vrajaḥ — Vrajabhūmi
      Recurs from verse 6, but now in its full theological weight. Vraja is the eternal mādhurya-dhāma — Gauḍīyas insist it is not merely a place where Kṛṣṇa performed pastimes long ago but the eternal location of His most intimate līlā, simultaneously present in the spiritual sky and on earth.
    • sarva-samṛddhimān — opulent with all kinds of riches
      Sarva + samṛddhi + mat: 'possessing complete prosperity.' The compound names Vraja's six opulences (aiśvarya) — but in Vraja these opulences are subordinated to mādhurya, the rasa that makes the Lord appear as everyone's family member.
    • hareḥ nivāsa — residence of the Supreme Lord Hari
      Hari from √hṛ ('to take away, to steal') — He who steals the heart. Nivāsa from ni + √vas ('to dwell'). Vraja is the eternal dwelling of the One who steals hearts — and so the verse anchors Vraja's status not in geography but in the Lord's constant residence there.
    • ātma-guṇaiḥ — by His own transcendental qualities
      Ātmā + guṇa. The opulence is not Vraja's by accident — it is the natural radiation of Bhagavān's own qualities into His abode. Acintya-bhedābheda: Vraja is non-different from Kṛṣṇa, yet also different enough to be the stage of His līlā.
    • ramā-ākrīḍam — the playground of the goddess of fortune
      Ramā (Lakṣmī) + ākrīḍa ('play-ground'). Even Lakṣmī, who normally resides only in Vaikuṇṭha, plays in Vraja. The Gauḍīya teaching: Vraja-aiśvarya exceeds Vaikuṇṭha-aiśvarya because the Lord of Lakṣmī Himself takes birth there.
    O Mahārāja Parīkṣit, the home of Nanda Mahārāja is eternally the abode of the Supreme Personality of Godhead and His transcendental qualities and is therefore always naturally endowed with the opulence of all wealth. Yet beginning from Lord Kṛṣṇa’s appearance there, it became the place for the pastimes of the goddess of fortune.
    Purport

    As stated in the Brahma-saṁhitā (5.29) , lakṣmī-sahasra-śata-sambhrama-sevyamānaṁ govindam ādi-puruṣaṁ tam ahaṁ bhajāmi. The abode of Kṛṣṇa is always served by hundreds and thousands of goddesses of fortune. Wherever Kṛṣṇa goes, the goddess of fortune naturally resides with Him. The chief of the goddesses of fortune is Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī. Therefore, Kṛṣṇa’s appearance in the land of Vraja indicated that the chief goddess of fortune, Rādhārāṇī, would also appear there very soon. Nanda Mahārāja’s abode was already opulent, and since Kṛṣṇa had appeared, it would be opulent in all respects.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    O Mahārāja Parīkṣit, the home of Nanda Mahārāja is eternally the abode of the Supreme Personality of Godhead and His transcendental qualities and is therefore always naturally endowed with the opulence of all wealth. Yet beginning from Lord Kṛṣṇa's appearance there, it became the place for the pastimes of the goddess of fortune.
    Commentary

    It is impossible for even Kuvera to satisfy everyone’s desires. How then was it possible for Nanda Maharaja to accomplish this? This verse answers the question. Krsna’s eternal place of residence, Vraja, is always increasing in Krsna’s qualities by its very nature. This started from the birth of Krsna. (tata arabhya). It became the place of pastimes for all wealth. If all wealth began to play in Nanda’s house, how was it possible that anything was lacking to give in charity?

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    “How was it possible to immediately decorate two million cows, make seven mountains of sesame and give in charity unlimited ornaments, cloth and decorated cows?” Śukadeva has said: rājadhānī tataḥ sābhūt sarva-yādava-bhūbhujām mathurā bhagavān yatra nityaṁ sannihito hariḥ Since that time, the city of Mathurā had been the capital of all the kings of the Yadu dynasty. The city and district of Mathurā are very intimately connected with Kṛṣṇa, for Kṛṣṇa lives there eternally. SB 10.1.28 jayati jana-nivāso devakī-janma-vādo yadu-vara-pariṣat svair dorbhir asyann adharmam sthira-cara-vṛjina-ghnaḥ su-smita-śrī-mukhena vraja-pura-vanitānāṁ vardhayan kāma-devam Kṛṣṇa lives eternally among the cowherd men and the Yādavas, and is conclusively both the son of Devakī and Yaśodā. He is the guide of the Yadu dynasty and the cowherd men, and with his mighty arms he kills everything inauspicious, in Vraja, Mathurā and Dvārakā. By his presence he destroys all things inauspicious for all living entities, moving and inert, and the suffering of separation of the inhabitants of Vraja and Dvārakā. His blissful smiling face always increases the desires of the gopīs of Vṛndāvana and women of Mathurā and Dvārakā. He remains eternally in this situation. SB 10.90.48 Śukadeva calls Kṛṣṇa “Indra of the cows” (SB 10.26.25) and “Lord of Gokula” (SB 10.10.39) Gopāla-tāpanī Upaniṣad says yo ‘sau gopeṣu tiṣṭhati: he remains eternally among the cowherds. From his birth Vraja became prosperous with the essential (ātma) qualities inherent in the Lord’s abode and became the place of pastimes of Lakṣmī. Bhagavān is served by his eternal consorts according to Brahma-saṁhitā 29: cintāmaṇi-prakara-sadmasu kalpa-vṛkṣa- lakṣāvṛteṣu surabhīr abhipālayantam | lakṣmī-sahasra-śata-sambhrama-sevyamānaṁ govindam ādi-puruṣaṁ tam ahaṁ bhajāmi || I worship the Supreme Lord Govinda who affectionately tends the cows in stables whose walls are made of cintāmaṇi, surrounded by desire trees. He is eagerly served by countless gopīs. nāyaṁ śriyo ’ṅga u nitānta-rateḥ prasādaḥ svar-yoṣitāṁ nalina-gandha-rucāṁ kuto ’nyāḥ rāsotsave ’sya bhuja-daṇḍa-gṛhīta-kaṇṭha- labdhāśiṣāṁ ya udagād vraja-vallabhīnām When Kṛṣṇa was dancing with the gopīs in the rāsa-līlā, the gopīs were embraced by the arms of the Lord. This transcendental favor was never bestowed upon the goddess of fortune or other consorts in the spiritual world. Indeed, never was such a thing even imagined by the most beautiful girls in the heavenly planets, whose bodily luster and aroma resemble the lotus flower. And what to speak of worldly women who are very beautiful according to material estimation? SB 10.47.60 This place became his place of pastimes because of the appearance of Rādhā, the supreme consort among all the gopīs of Vraja, who were far superior to the women of heaven and to Lakṣmī in Vaikuṇṭha. The sentence can also mean “Vraja, becoming prosperous from the time of Kṛṣṇa’s birth, became the residence of pastimes with the gopīs with manifestation of the essential qualities of Lord’s abode.” What to speak of Vraja becoming most prosperous from Kṛṣṇa’s birth there, it became the place of pastimes of his consorts with the essential qualities of the Lord’s own abode. What is impossible at this time since all prosperity suddenly manifests just by the glance of Lakṣmī of the whole universe (Rādhā). In this place there were abodes of cintāmaṇi for their confidential pastimes. This will be explained in the twenty-eighth chapter. This of course implies that the young gopīs also took birth at this time. Taking the meaning of ramākrīḍam to mean all wealth would be repetitive since samṛddhimān was already mentioned. And to say that the phrase means “pastimes with a woman other than Rādhā” would be vague. Otherwise the word ātmā would be meaningless in the phrase harer nivāsātma-guṇaiḥ. Other interpretations are thus rejected.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    “How was it possible to immediately decorate two million cows, make seven mountains of sesame and give in charity unlimited ornaments, cloth and decorated cows?” From his birth Vraja became prosperous with the essential (ātma) qualities (such as generosity and devotion) inherent in the Lord’s abode and became the place of pastimes of Lakṣmī. The cause was the Lord (hareḥ). There were buildings of cintāmaṇi, desire trees and kāmadhenus. O king, this wealth is not possible even for a king like you. Or Śukadev calls out in astonishment.

    Personal Guidance
    Wherever Kṛṣṇa is welcomed, Lakṣmī follows — invite Him into your home and the true opulence (and Rādhārāṇī's mood of service) arrives with Him.
    Practical Applications
    • Before chasing any new acquisition today, pause and ask whether you are inviting Lakṣmī directly or inviting Kṛṣṇa, in whose presence she always resides.
    • Treat your home as Nanda's Vraja: clean one corner, install Kṛṣṇa there, and offer the day's first food to Him before you eat.
    • Refuse the inversion of seeking fortune apart from Kṛṣṇa — when prosperity comes, immediately re-offer it to Him as the source.
    Analogy

    Where the Ganges enters a city, the river makes that city auspicious forever — not because the city had no waters before, but because something new and inexhaustible has settled there. Vraja was already Bhagavān's eternal abode; Kṛṣṇa's appearance there in this manifest līlā makes it now the playground of Lakṣmī herself.

    Stories
    The Six Gosvāmīs recovering Vraja

    Five thousand years after the events of this chapter, Vraja had been lost — buried under jungle, forgotten by all but a few. Caitanya Mahāprabhu sent Rūpa and Sanātana Gosvāmīs to Vṛndāvana with the specific charge of recovering the holy places of Kṛṣṇa's līlā.

    They, joined by Jīva, Gopāla Bhaṭṭa, Raghunātha Bhaṭṭa, and Raghunātha dāsa, identified Govardhana, Rādhā-kuṇḍa, Śyāma-kuṇḍa, and the twelve forests of Vraja; established temples; and wrote the theological literature that explained why Vraja is not a geography but the eternal hareḥ nivāsa this verse names. The Gosvāmīs' work is the historical answer to this verse's claim — Vraja is always Bhagavān's residence, and even when buried, He arranges its recovery.

    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.5.19

    ↗
    गोपान् गोकुलरक्षायां निरूप्य मथुरां गत: । नन्द: कंसस्य वार्षिक्यं करं दातुं कुरूद्वह ॥ १९ ॥
    gopān gokula-rakṣāyāṁ

    the share of profit

    to pay
    kuru - udvaha
    O Mahārāja Parīkṣit, best protector of the Kuru dynasty .
    Important Words
    • gokula-rakṣāyām — in giving protection to Gokula
      Gokula (go + kula, 'the family of cows') + rakṣā ('protection'). Nanda's first act before leaving is to appoint protection for Gokula — a king does not leave his realm unguarded. Gauḍīya teaching: a gṛhastha's responsibilities extend beyond his own family to the saṅga and the community.
    • mathurām — to Mathurā
      Mathurā: the kingdom's seat under Kaṁsa, and later one of the seven mokṣa-dā cities. The verse marks the geographical tension of the chapter — Vraja (Kṛṣṇa's true home) versus Mathurā (the political center) — which will resolve only when Kṛṣṇa Himself returns to Mathurā years later to kill Kaṁsa.
    • vārṣikyam-karam — yearly tax
      Vārṣika (annual) + kara ('tax'). The same word kara also means 'hand' — what one renders by hand. Even under a tyrant, the dharmic householder renders his hand-payment; he does not rebel for personal convenience. The kingdom's protection of cows depends on this dharmic discipline.
    • dātum — to pay
      Infinitive of √dā ('to give'). Even tax-paying is a form of dāna in the Vedic mood — the verb refuses the modern grudge. Nanda gives the tax as he gives charity: as part of his dharma.
    • kuru-udvaha — O best protector of the Kuru dynasty
      Vocative addressed to Parīkṣit. Śukadeva is reminding the dying king that his ancestors, the Pāṇḍavas, were also protectors of dharma under hostile circumstances — Parīkṣit must hear Nanda's story as kin-history, not legend.
    Śukadeva Gosvāmī continued: Thereafter, my dear King Parīkṣit, O best protector of the Kuru dynasty, Nanda Mahārāja appointed the local cowherd men to protect Gokula and then went to Mathurā to pay the yearly taxes to King Kaṁsa.
    Purport

    Because the killing of babies was going on and had already become known, Nanda Mahārāja was very much afraid for his newborn child. Thus he appointed the local cowherd men to protect his home and child. He wanted to go immediately to Mathurā to pay the taxes due and also to offer some presentation for the sake of his newborn son. For the protection of the child, he had worshiped various demigods and forefathers and given charity to everyone’s satisfaction. Similarly, Nanda Mahārāja wanted not only to pay Kaṁsa the yearly taxes but also to offer some presentation so that Kaṁsa too would be satisfied. His only concern was how to protect his transcendental child, Kṛṣṇa.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Śukadeva Gosvāmī continued: Thereafter, my dear King Parīkṣit, O best protector of the Kuru dynasty, Nanda Mahārāja appointed the local cowherd men to protect Gokula and then went to Mathurā to pay the yearly taxes to King Kaṁsa.
    Commentary

    Many days after the birth of his son, thinking of his son as a great treasure, Nanda began to think, " In all good fortune there are many impediments." First satisfying the devas, pitrs and planets with worship, without delay he then departed for Mathura with gifts of gold coins, jewels and cloth to give to the evil king Kamsa as yearly tax to please him.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Previously because of lack of a son Nanda had no possessiveness of wealth and had no fear of Kaṁsa. With the birth of a precious son he became anxious to protect him, worrying greatly. He therefore personally went quickly to Mathurā city to ally insurmountable fears. He engaged cowherds in protecting Vraja. He had to give yearly taxes at the beginning of the year starting in Śrāvana month. Śukadeva addresses Parīkṣit as Kurūdvaha, leader of the Kurus, to show that Yudhiṣṭhira was also anxious to protect the remaining son in his dynasty. (Similarly Nanda wanted to protect Vraja.)

    Personal Guidance
    Even the simplest worldly duty — paying taxes — becomes yajña when its hidden motive is the protection of Kṛṣṇa.
    Practical Applications
    • Before today's most ordinary obligation (a bill, a meeting, an errand), silently dedicate it as raksha for Kṛṣṇa and those entrusted to you.
    • Notice when fear for what you love sharpens your udyama — let that vigilance, not anxiety, drive your effort today.
    • Refuse to abandon worldly responsibilities in the name of devotion; like Nanda, discharge them precisely so the inner treasure stays guarded.
    Analogy

    Even the father of the Lord pays taxes to a usurper. Bhakti does not exempt the devotee from worldly responsibility — it places him precisely inside it, with a heart that no Kaṁsa can claim. Nanda riding to Mathurā with the king's annual tax is the model gṛhastha: render to Caesar, then ride home to Kṛṣṇa.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.5.20

    ↗
    वसुदेव उपश्रुत्य भ्रातरं नन्दमागतम् । ज्ञात्वा दत्तकरं राज्ञे ययौ तदवमोचनम् ॥ २० ॥
    vasudeva upaśrutya
    [vasudeva upashrutya]
    bhrātaraṁ nandam āgatam
    [bhraataram nandam aagatam]
    jñātvā datta-karaṁ rājñe
    [jnaatvaa datta-karam raajne]
    yayau tad-avamocanam
    [yayau tad-avamochanam]
    Word-for-word
    vasudevaḥ — Vasudeva upaśrutya — when he heard bhrātaram — that his dear friend and brother nandam — Nanda Mahārāja āgatam — had come to Mathurā jñātvā — when he learned datta - karam — and had already paid the taxes rājñe — unto the King yayau — he went tat - avamocanam — to the residential quarters of Nanda Mahārāja .

    to the residential quarters of Nanda Mahārāja .
    Important Words
    • vasudevaḥ — Vasudeva
      From vasu ('wealth, divine being') + deva. The biological father of Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma. Gauḍīya theology distinguishes between Vasudeva (the Mathurā-father in aiśvarya-mood) and Nanda (the Vraja-father in pure vātsalya-mood) — both are real fatherhoods, expressing two different rasas.
    • upaśrutya — having heard
      Upa + √śru: 'having heard nearby.' The same root as śravaṇa — the first limb of bhakti. Even the bare hearing that Nanda has come is enough to set Vasudeva in motion. Hearing of Kṛṣṇa-associated persons is itself a beginning of bhakti.
    • bhrātaram — brother
      From √bhrātṛ. Vasudeva calls Nanda 'brother' — they are related through the line of King Śūrasena (Mathurā tradition; SB and Hari-vaṁśa). The verse establishes that Kṛṣṇa's two fathers are themselves kin, which Gauḍīya ācāryas read as further evidence that vātsalya in Vraja and aiśvarya in Mathurā are one extended family.
    • jñātvā — knowing
      Gerund of √jñā ('to know'). Vasudeva does not go to Nanda until he knows the tax has been paid — the Vaiṣṇava is sensitive to others' obligations, never imposing his eagerness on someone else's duty. The discrimination is itself a virtue.
    • tat-avamocanam — to Nanda's lodging
      Tat ('that, his') + avamocana ('place of unyoking,' from ava + √muc). A lodging is where the traveler unyokes his bullocks. The technical word reminds us that Nanda has come with cows and carts, and Vasudeva is going to meet him in his traveler's quarters, not in a palace.
    When Vasudeva heard that Nanda Mahārāja, his very dear friend and brother, had come to Mathurā and already paid the taxes to Kaṁsa, he went to Nanda Mahārāja’s residence.
    Purport

    Vasudeva and Nanda Mahārāja were so intimately connected that they lived like brothers. Furthermore, it is learned from the notes of Śrīpāda Madhvācārya that Vasudeva and Nanda Mahārāja were stepbrothers. Vasudeva’s father, Śūrasena, married a vaiśya girl, and from her Nanda Mahārāja was born. Later, Nanda Mahārāja himself married a vaiśya girl, Yaśodā. Therefore his family is celebrated as a vaiśya family, and Kṛṣṇa, identifying Himself as their son, took charge of vaiśya activities ( kṛṣi-go-rakṣya-vāṇijyam ). Balarāma represents plowing the land for agriculture and therefore always carries in His hand a plow, whereas Kṛṣṇa tends cows and therefore carries a flute in His hand. Thus the two brothers represent kṛṣi-rakṣya and go-rakṣya.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    When Vasudeva heard that Nanda Mahārāja, his very dear friend and brother, had come to Mathurā and already paid the taxes to Kaṁsa, he went to Nanda Mahārāja's residence.
    Commentary

    King Devamidha of the Yadu clan (Yadava) had two wives, one of ksatriya family, the other of vaisya family. Through the ksatriya wife he bore a son names Sura and through the vaisya wife he bore a son named Parjanya. Sura had a son named Vasudeva, a ksatriya, and Parjanya had a son named Nanda, a cowherd. Thus Vasudeva and Nanda were brothers or cousins, with the same grandfather (a Yadava) and different grandmother. Nanda was therefore a Yadava. In Skanda Purana Krsna says, "I have lifted Govardhana for the benefit of the Yadavas (Nanda and others)." And Krsna says to his brother, "Among all the Yadavas (both Mathura relatives and Vraja relatives), you are the most dear." Tad avamocanam means the place where Nanda was residing. (avamoca-to loosen the clothes).

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Vasudeva heard from others that Nanda had come, since Nanda was respected and known to all people. Through spies he learned that Nanda had paid the taxes. Otherwise he would have been uncomfortable in any meeting with him because of anxiety. Nanda is called him brother because Devamīḍha gave birth to Vasudeva’s father Śūra through a kṣatriya wife and to Nanda’s father Parjanya through a vaiśya wife. Brahmā says: tasmai māyā sa varaḥ saṁnisṛṣṭaḥ sa cāsa nandākhya utāsya bhāryā nāmnā yaśodā sa ca śūratāta- sutasya vaiśyāprabhavo ‘tha gopaḥ I have the boon to Nanda whose wife was named Yaśodā. He was a cowherd coming through Śūra’s father (Devamīḍha) and a vaiśya mother. In that sense Nanda was his brother. This is a general reference. In Skanda Purāṇa, Mathurā-māhāmya, discussing Govardhana their relationship as brothers is expressed (by calling the cowherds Yādavas): govardhanaś ca bhagavān yatra govardhano dhṛtaḥ rakṣitā yādava sarve indra-vṛṣṭi-nivāranāt When Kṛṣṇa lifted Govardhana he protected the Yādavas from the rain of Indra. However they are considered vaiśyas because Nanda’s grandmother was a vaiśya. In the Skanda Purāṇa the Lord says yādvānāṁ hitārthāya dhṛto giririvaro mayā: I lifted Govardhana for the benefit of the Yādavas. In Hari-vaṁśa, Rāma speaks to his brothers. Yādaveṣvapi sarveṣu bhavanto mama vallabhā: the cowherds are most dear to me among all the Yādavas. Avamocana means the place where the bulls were released from the carts. Longing for news of his son, Vasudeva went to Nanda with a desire to send him back quickly to Vraja to protect his son.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Vasudeva heard from others that Nanda had come, since the news spread. He learned that Nanda had paid the taxes to Kaṁsa. Otherwise he would have been uncomfortable in any meeting with him because of anxiety. Or he heard because Nanda became famous for giving tax in the form of abundant milk products. He went to the place where Nanda parked the carts out of fear of Kaṁsa. Just as Nanda was coming, Vasudeva came quickly out of affection for his son.
